iv. The domestic industry has proposed the PCN methodology determined in the original investigation, 
excluding PCNs A-I-I to A-l-4. 


Vv. The goods produced by the domestic industry and that imported into India are like article. There is no 
known difference in the product produced by the applicants and those imported from the subject 
country. 


Examination by the Authority 


The product under consideration in the original investigation was “Seamless tubes, pipes & hollow profiles of 
iron, alloy or non-alloy steel (other than cast iron and stainless steel), whether hot finished or cold drawn or cold 
rolled of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or |4” OD”, including boiler pipes or line pipes used in 
hydrocarbon industry and casing and tubing of a kind used in drilling for oil and gas exploration., subject to the 
following exclusions — 


i. Seamless pipes and tubes made of cast iron and stainless steel 


il. | Seamless alloy-steel pipes, tubes and hollow profiles of specifications ASTM A2l3/ASME SA 23 and 
ASTM A335/ASME SA 335 or equivalent 


ill. Non-API and Premium Joints/Premium Connections/Premium Threaded Tubes & Pipes as prescribed 
under Customs Notification No. 2/202 dated 7 March 202 at S. No. 356 


iv. All 3 Chromium (I3CR) grade tubes and pipes 
५. Drill collars 


vi. High pressure seamless steel pipe/tube used for manufacturing gas cylinders by producers approved by 
the Chief Controller of Explosives, PESO. 


At the request of the applicants, the Authority, at the stage of initiation itself, restricted the scope of the product 
under consideration for the purpose of the present review by excluding () seamless tubing, (ii) seamless casing, 
of a kind used in drilling for oil or gas, (iii) seamless mother hollows, coupling stock, blanks/pup joints, and 
(iv) seamless drill pipes, of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or |4” OD (i.e., OCTG products 
covered under PCNs A-I-I to A-l-4 in the original investigation). The phrase “casing and tubing of a kind 
used in drilling for oil and gas exploration” in the original description stands consequentially excluded by the 
express exclusion of such goods, and the omission in the domestic industry’s comments does not enlarge or 
otherwise alter the scope as notified. 


The Authority notes that neither the Act nor the Rules bar the curtailment of the scope of the product under 
consideration in a sunset review, particularly where the domestic industry itself does not seek continuation of 
protection in respect of certain product types. The curtailment reduces, rather than enlarges, the coverage of the 
measure and causes no prejudice to the exporters or users. The Authority has consistently followed this 
approach in past sunset reviews. The contention of JSC is, therefore, not accepted. 


Accordingly, the product under consideration in the present review, as also notified at the stage of initiation 
vide Notification No. 7/08/2026-DGTR, dated 20 March 2026, is as below. Further, the Authority also 
notified the same product scope as notified at the time of initiation along with an additional clarification vide 
notice dated 77 May 2026 following submissions received from all interested parties on the issue of product 
scope and PCN. 


“The product under consideration in the present investigation is “Seamless tubes, pipes & hollow profiles of 
iron, alloy or non-alloy steel (other than cast iron and stainless steel), whether hot finished or cold drawn or 
cold rolled of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or /47 OD”. It includes boiler pipes or line pipes 
used in hydrocarbon industry. 


The following products are excluded from the scope of product under consideration: 
i. Seamless pipes & tubes made of cast iron and stainless steel. 


il. Seamless alloy-steel pipes, tubes and hollow profiles of specifications of ASTM 427/3/450/7 SA 2/3 and 
ASTM A335/ ASME SA 335 or equivalent BIS/DIN/BS/EN or any other equivalent specifications. 


ili. Non - API and Premium Joints / Premium Connections / Premium Threaded Tubes & Pipes as 
prescribed under Customs Notification No. 72/2072, dated 777 March 2072 at serial number 356. 


79. 4843 Chromium (73070 Grade Tubes and Pipes. 
9. Drill Collars.

3. 


vi. High pressure seamless steel pipe/tube used for manufacturing gas cylinders by producers approved by 
the Chief Controller of Explosives, Petroleum and Explosives Safety Organisation, Government of 
India. 


vil. Seamless Tubing, of a kind used in drilling for oil or gas, Carbon/ Non- Alloy/ Alloy, hot finished or 
cold drawn or cold rolled of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or /47 OD. 


vill. Seamless Casing, of a kind used in drilling for oil or gas, Carbon/Non -Alloy/ Alloy, hot finished or cold 
drawn or cold rolled of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or /47 OD. 


ix. Seamless Mother Hollows, coupling stock, blanks/ Pup Joints, Carbon/ Non Alloy/ Alloy, hot finished or 
cold drawn or cold rolled of an external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or /47 OD. 


x. Seamless Drill Pipes, of a kind used in drilling for oil or gas, Carbon/Non alloy, hot finished of an 
external diameter not exceeding 355.6 mm or /47” OD”.” 


The product under consideration is classified under heading 7304 of Chapter 73 of the First Schedule to the 
Customs Tariff Act, !975. The customs classification is indicative only and not binding on the scope of the 
product under consideration.

As regards like article, the domestic industry has claimed that there is no known difference between the subject 
goods produced by it and those imported from the subject country in terms of physical and chemical 
characteristics, manufacturing process and technology, functions and uses, product specifications, pricing, 
distribution and marketing, and tariff classification. No comments were made by the other interested party in 
this regard. The Authority holds that the subject goods produced by the domestic industry are like article to the 
product under consideration imported from the subject country within the meaning of Rule 2(d) of the Rules. 


D. SCOPE OF DOMESTIC INDUSTRY AND STANDING 
Submissions by other interested parties 


JSC has submitted that the constitution of the domestic industry has changed across the original investigation, 
the first sunset review and the present review without explanation; that ISMT Limited has been dropped while 
Kirloskar Ferrous Industries Limited has been introduced for the first time and Maharashtra Seamless Limited 
re-introduced; and that such curation undermines the representativeness of the injury analysis. JSC has sought a 
reasoned justification for the exclusion of ISMT Limited. 


Submissions by the domestic industry 


The domestic industry has submitted that the applicants are the three largest producers of the subject goods in 
India, accounting for 93% of the Indian production, that they have neither imported the subject goods from the 
subject country nor are related to any exporter or importer of the subject goods. It was submitted that ISMT 


56 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


Limited, an applicant in the earlier proceedings, underwent liquidation and was acquired by Kirloskar Ferrous 
Industries Limited. Further, there is no legal obligation to maintain the same constitution of the domestic 
industry across proceedings, as held by the Authority in the first sunset review itself. 


D.3. Examination by the Authority 
7. Rule 2(b) of the Rules defines domestic industry as the domestic producers as a whole engaged in the 
manufacture of the like article, or those whose collective output constitutes a major proportion of the total 
domestic production, excluding producers related to exporters or importers or themselves importers of the 
alleged dumped article. 
8. The Authority notes that there are six other known producers of the subject goods in India besides the 
applicants. The production of the applicants and other Indian producers during the POI is as follows:

The applicants have certified that they have not imported the subject goods from the subject country during the 
injury period and are not related to any exporter or importer of the subject goods. The Authority notes that the 
applicants account for 93% of the total Indian production of the like article and therefore, constitute a major 
proportion of the total domestic production in India. 


As regards the change in constitution of the domestic industry, the Authority notes that ISMT Limited, as 
submitted by the domestic industry, underwent liquidation after the first sunset review and was acquired by 
Kirloskar Ferrous Industries Limited. Thus, ISMT Limited is no longer in existence as a separate producer of 
the subject goods. Further, it is noted that there is no legal obligation on the Authority or the applicants to 
maintain the same constitution of the domestic industry in successive proceedings. The Authority is only 
required to ascertain that the applicants satisfy the requirements of Rule 2(b). In the present case, the applicants 
represent 93% of Indian production and thus, the question of selective curation does not arise. The contention 
of JSC is, therefore, not accepted. 


In view of the above, the Authority finds that the applicants constitute the domestic industry within the 
meaning of Rule 2(b) of the Rules and that the application satisfies the criteria of standing under Rule 5(3) of 
the Rules. 

E. CONFIDENTIALITY 
Submissions by other interested parties 


The sole responding exporter, JSC, has submitted response to exporter’s questionnaire and made other 
submissions, wherein certain information has been claimed confidential after providing reasons for the same. 


Submissions by domestic industry 


The domestic industry has made various submissions during the investigation, and has claimed certain 
information as confidential after providing reasons for the same. 


Examination by the Authority 


The non-confidential version of the information provided by the interested parties was made available to all 
interested parties as per Rule 6(7) and Trade Notice 0/208 dated 7" September 208 read with Trade Notice 
/203 dated 97 December 203. 


“Confidential information: (7) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-rules (2), (3) and (7) of rule 
6, sub-rule(2) of rulel2,sub-rule(4) of rule ॥र and sub-rule (4) of rule /7, the copies of applications 
received under sub-rule (7) of rule 5, or any other information provided to the designated authority on a 
confidential basis by any party in the course of investigation, shall, upon the designated authority being 


[भाग aus ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 57 


25. 


26. 


&.. 
27. 


satisfied as to its confidentiality, be treated as such by it and no such information shall be disclosed to 
any other party without specific authorization of the party providing such information. 


(2) The designated authority may require the parties providing information on a confidential basis to 
furnish a non-confidential summary thereof and if, in the opinion of a party providing such information, 
such information is not susceptible to summary, such party may submit to the designated authority a 
statement of reasons why summarization is not possible. 


(3) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-rule (2), if the designated authority is satisfied that the 
request for confidentiality is not warranted or the supplier of the information is either unwilling to make 
the information public or to authorise its disclosure in a generalized or summary form, it may disregard 
such information.” 


The claims made by the interested parties, to the extent considered relevant, were examined by the Authority 
and addressed accordingly. It is seen that the domestic industry has claimed confidentiality on various 
information, such as, production, capacity, capacity utilization, sales volumes, market share, stocks, selling 
price, costs, profits, cash profits, return on investment, non-injurious price, cost of production related 
information, normal value based on cost of production, dumping margin, injury margin, etc. It is also seen that 
wherever information is for the injury period, the same has been provided on an indexed basis. Information 
such as normal value, non-injurious price and price undercutting has been disclosed in the range. Further, the 
domestic industry has also claimed confidentiality on various supporting documents & information, wherever 
such information has not been publicly disclosed by them. The domestic industry has claimed that non- 
confidential summary of such information cannot be provided since these documents are not susceptible to 
summary. Similarly, the exporter has claimed confidentiality with respect to its sales volume, sales value, 
prices, cost of production and supporting documentation. Where possible, the exporter has provided 
information in indexed form. 


The information provided by the interested parties on a confidential basis was examined with regard to 
sufficiency of such claims. On being satisfied, the Authority has accepted the confidentiality claims, wherever 
warranted, and such information has been considered confidential and not disclosed to other interested parties. 
Wherever possible, the interested parties were directed to provide sufficient non confidential versions of the 
information filed on a confidential basis. 


F. MISCELLANEOUS SUBMISSIONS 
Submissions by other interested parties 


The following miscellaneous submissions have been made by the other interested parties. 


J, Article .3 of the Anti-Dumping Agreement mandates expiry of duty after five years as the rule, and 
continuation is an exception requiring a rigorous examination based on positive evidence (Appellate 
Body in US — Corrosion-Resistant Steel Sunset Review; CESTAT in All India Laminated Fabrics 
Manufacturers Association v. Designated Authority). The duties have been in force for over ten years, 
and the growth of the Indian industry from 5 to |0 producers shows that the duty has served its purpose. 


il. Section 9A(5) of the Act only empowers the Central Government to ‘extend’ the existing duty and does 
not contemplate re-determination of the quantum or form of duty in a sunset review. In a number of 
recent sunset reviews (Faced Glass Wool, Methyl Acetoacetate, Flexible Slabstock Polyol, 2-Ethyl 
Hexanol, FKM, Flax Fabric, Aniline, Sodium Citrate, DOPP, EVA Sheet, etc.), the Authority continued 
the same duty for cooperating producers as determined in the original investigation, even though fresh 
margins were quantified. 


ili. | The request for enhancement of the reference price is a recycled argument that was rejected in the first 
sunset review and not challenged. The reference price is denominated in US$ at the instance of the 
domestic industry itself; owing to depreciation of the INR (= 6.59/US$ in the original POI to = 87/US$ 
in the present POI), the reference price in rupee terms has already increased by about 55%. If the 
reference price is revisited, it should be reduced in US$ terms or fixed in INR. 


iv. JSC, which has cooperated in the original investigation, the first sunset review and the present review, 
should be granted an individual rate, and the same rate and form of duty should be continued for it. A 
higher duty may be prescribed for ‘all others’. 


Vv. The allegations of over-invoicing and transshipment are unsubstantiated and sweeping and do not 
concern JSC. 


58 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SECc.]]

Submissions by the domestic industry

XVII. 


Under Article .3 of the Anti-Dumping Agreement and Section 9A(5) of the Act, duties are to be 
continued where the Authority determines that expiry of duty is likely to lead to continuation or 
recurrence of dumping and injury. There is no requirement to establish any ‘exceptional’ circumstance. 


The CESTAT in All India Laminated Fabrics Manufacturers Association vs. DA held that the duties can 
be continued if the Authority, having conducted rigorous investigation, found likelihood of continuation 
or recurrence of dumping and injury. 


The Authority has continued duties in second, third and fourth sunset reviews in several cases, such as 
Acrylic Fibre, Caustic Soda, Phenol, Melamine, PTFE, Nylon Tyre Cord Fabric, etc. In fact, various 
other jurisdictions have also extended duties for more than 20 years, where warranted. 


The present duties are in the form of a reference price fixed on the basis of the cost and price conditions 
of January—-December 20l4. The landed price of the subject imports during the POI is above the 
reference price but below the current non-injurious price for PCNs A-l-5 and A-l-6, and as long as 
imports are priced marginally above the reference price, they continue to cause injury. The reference 
price form has thus been rendered ineffective. 


While the request for modification of form and quantum of duty was not accepted in the previous 
review, the facts and circumstances of the present case are different and warrant such modifications. 


The Authority has modified the form of duty from reference price to fixed duty in the past (Caustic 
Soda, CPVC, Measuring Tapes, Melamine, MDF, PTFE, Phenol, Sodium Nitrite, Viscose Filament 
Yarn). The Authority has also enhanced the quantum of duty in sunset reviews (Caustic Soda, PTFE, 
Atrazine (CVD), Glass Fibre). 


The exporters in the subject country are resorting to unfair selling practices in order to avoid payment of 
duties. 


Since the reference price duty is triggered only if the price of imports is lower than the fixed limit, the 
Chinese exporters are declaring an export price which is marginally higher than the benchmark 
determined by the Authority, in order to avoid paying the anti-dumping duties. 


However, such price is only being declared at the Customs port and is not the price which is offered to 
the customers. The actual price offered to the customers is much lower than the price declared at 
customs port. 


Such low-priced imports are eventually entering the market and competing with the domestic industry. 


The Chinese exporters are also resorting to transshipment of subject goods through third countries such 
as the UAE and Singapore. 


Detailed market research reports submitted show that there are no production facilities in such countries 
and all exports are being made only through traders. 


Significant volume of exports in absence of any production facilities clearly shows that the traders in 
third countries are selling Chinese goods to avoid duty payment. 


The volume and price of such imports should be considered as part of subject imports. 


The duty should be denominated in US$/MT, since exporters transact in US$; duty in INR would be 
rendered ineffective with depreciation of the rupee. 


The exchange rate has not protected the domestic industry against raw material price fluctuations. 


Individual duty for the cooperating exporter may be determined upon verification, but on the basis of 
the dumping margin and injury margin for the present POI, and not by merely continuing the earlier 
duty. Any differential treatment whereby the old duty is continued for the cooperating exporter while 
duty is modified for others would be discriminatory. 


Examination by the Authority 


As regards the contention that continuation of duty is an exception, the Authority notes that Section 9A(5) of 
the Act and Rule 23(/B) of the Rules, consistent with Article l.3 of the Anti-Dumping Agreement, provide 
that the duty shall cease after five years unless the Authority, in a review, comes to the conclusion that the 
expiry of duty is likely to lead to continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. Neither the statute nor the 


[भाग I—ars ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 59 


29. 


30. 


Sk. 


32. 


33. 


jurisprudence relied upon prescribes any limit on the number of reviews or require the establishment of 
circumstances beyond a positive likelihood determination based on an objective examination of positive 
evidence. The Authority has undertaken such an examination in the present case, as set out in the following 
sections. The Authority notes that even when anti-dumping duty has been in place for about l0 years, the 
volume of imports in the present period is significantly high in both absolute terms and in relation to 
production and consumption in India. Further, the capacity utilization of the domestic industry is quite low, 
thus showing gross underutilisation of production capacities because of presence of dumped imports in the 
country. Economic performance of the domestic industry has shown deterioration in the present period, as is 
examined in detail in relevant paragraphs herein. 


The domestic industry has placed market research report on record, which shows (a) absence of production 
facilities in these countries, (b) name of the companies who have exported the product to India (based on 
market source data on imports), and (c) the nature of operations of the trading company. Further, the domestic 
industry has also placed on record a report of the U.S Trade Representative titled “The Great Transshipment 
Scam” where it is noted that various third countries exporting the subject goods to India are regularly engaging 
in transhipment of Chinese goods. The matter has been examined. As regards the claim of transhipment, the 
Authority notes that the import data procured from DG Systems records imports of the product under 
consideration declared as originating in UAE and Singapore. While the domestic industry has placed on record 
market research regarding the absence of production facilities in these countries and the trading nature of the 
exporters, the Authority notes that determination of origin of the goods at the transaction level is a matter 
falling within the domain of the Customs authorities under the Customs Act, 962. The Authority has, for the 
purpose of the present determination, considered as subject imports only those imports recorded as originating 
in China PR. The domestic industry is at liberty to approach the Authority under Rule 25 of the Rules with duly 
substantiated evidence of circumvention. 


The domestic industry has requested for change in the form of duty in order to remedy the continued injury 
being caused by the dumped imports. The anti-dumping duty presently in force is in the form of a reference 
price, whereby duty equal to the difference between the reference price and the landed value of the imports is 
levied where the landed value is below the reference price. The domestic industry has submitted that the 
present duties have been ineffective in remedying the continued imports in significant volume despite duties in 
place for 0 years. Further, the domestic industry has claimed that due to the benchmark form of the duty, the 
Chinese exporters have aligned their prices to the reference price notified by the Authority. As such, the import 
prices being declared at the Customs port is at the level or slightly above the reference price, while the actual 
re-sale price offered to customers in the market is much lower. As a result, the domestic industry has contended 
that it has continued to face injury due to presence of low-priced imports. The other interested party has 
claimed that the form should not be modified since duties in the form of reference price was imposed at the 
request of the domestic industry. It has also been added that in case the duty is modified, JSC being the sole 
cooperative producer, should be given an individual rate. 


The Authority notes that the reference price now in force was set on the cost and price conditions of the 
original period of investigation (January—December 204). Those conditions are now more than a decade old. 
The record of the present review shows that most subject imports during the POI entered at landed values at or 
close to the reference price. This suggests that declared import prices may be gravitating towards the reference 
price rather than reflecting conditions in the market. At the same time, the domestic industry’s performance has 
deteriorated during the injury period, and the volume of subject imports has risen significantly. These facts 
raise a question about whether the present form of duty is still effective in removing the injury. 


The fact that the reference price form was adopted at the domestic industry’s request in the original 
investigation does not bind the Authority in this review. The form of duty is to be decided on the record of the 
present proceedings, and in light of how the measure has actually worked over its life. 


As regards the contention of JSC that the INR depreciation has increased the reference price by about 55% in 
rupee terms, the Authority notes that the exchange rate in the POI of the original investigation was 6.59, while 
the same was 73.l7 in the POI of the previous sunset review. The exchange rate in the present POI is 86.76. 
Thus, the exchange rate is increased by 4!% as compared to original investigation, and by !9% as compared to 
previous sunset review. It is thus incorrect that the change is 55%. Further, the relevant comparison is between 
the landed price of the imports and the current non-injurious price, both of which are determined afresh for the 
POI and in INR terms. Further, the majority of the imports in the present POI are at a price very close to the 
benchmark in place. The Authority also notes that duties denominated in US$ are consistent with its 
established practice, since the export transactions are in US$.

G. NORMAL VALUE, EXPORT PRICE AND DETERMINATION OF DUMPING MARGIN 


Submissions by other interested parties 


The other interested party has not made any submissions with reference to determination of normal value, 
export price and dumping margin. 


Submissions by the domestic industry 


The domestic industry has submitted that, in terms of Paragraphs 7 and 8 of Annexure-I to the Rules, the 
normal value for Chinese producers is required to be determined on the basis of the price or constructed value 
in a market economy third country, or where it is not possible, on any other reasonable basis, including the 
price actually paid or payable in India for the like product, duly adjusted to include a reasonable profit margin, 
unless the responding Chinese producers demonstrate that market economy conditions prevail. Since the sole 
cooperating producer / exporter has not claimed market economy treatment, normal value for the cooperating 
producer as well as all other producers / exporters should be determined as per Para 7 of Annexure-I of Anti- 
dumping Rules. 


Examination by the Authority 


Market economy treatment and normal value for China PR 
Article 5 of China’s Accession Protocol to the WTO provides as follows: 


“Article VI of the GATT 7994, the Agreement on Implementation of Article VI of the General Agreement 
on Tariffs and Trade 994 ("Anti-Dumping Agreement") and the SCM Agreement shall apply in 
proceedings involving imports of Chinese origin into a WTO Member consistent with the following: 


In determining price comparability under Article VI of the GATT 7994 and the Anti-Dumping 
Agreement, the importing WTO Member shall use either Chinese prices or costs for the industry under 
investigation or a methodology that is not based on a strict comparison with domestic prices or costs in 
China based on the following rules: 


(i) If the producers under investigation can clearly show that market economy conditions prevail in the 
industry producing the like product with regard to the manufacture, production and sale of that product, 
the importing WTO Member shall use Chinese prices or costs for the industry under investigation in 
determining price comparability; 


(ti) The importing WTO Member may use a methodology that is not based on a strict comparison with 
domestic prices or costs in China if the producers under investigation cannot clearly show that market 
economy conditions prevail in the industry producing the like product with regard to manufacture, 
production and sale of that product. 


In proceedings under Parts II, III and V of the SCM Agreement, when addressing subsidies described in 
Articles /4(9), /4(0), /4(०0) and /4(6), relevant provisions of the SCM Agreement shall apply; however, 
if there are special difficulties in that application, the importing WTO Member may then use 
methodologies for identifying and measuring the subsidy benefit which take into account the possibility 
that prevailing terms and conditions in China may not always be available as appropriate benchmarks. 
In applying such methodologies, where practicable, the importing WTO Member should adjust such 
prevailing terms and conditions before considering the use of terms and conditions prevailing outside 
China. 


The importing WTO Member shall notify methodologies used in accordance with subparagraph (a) to 
the Committee on Anti-Dumping Practices and shall notify methodologies used in accordance with 
subparagraph (b) to the Committee on Subsidies and Countervailing Measures. 


Once China has established, under the national law of the importing WTO Member, that it is a market 
economy, the provisions of subparagraph (a) shall be terminated provided that the importing Member's 
national law contains market economy criteria as of the date of accession. In any event, the provisions 
of subparagraph (a)(ti) shall expire 73 years after the date of accession. In addition, should China 
establish, pursuant to the national law of the importing WTO Member, that market economy conditions 
prevail in a particular industry or sector, the non-market economy provisions of subparagraph (a) shall 
no longer apply to that industry or sector.” 


The applicants have cited and relied upon Article l5(a)(i) of China’s Accession Protocol. The applicants have 
claimed that producers in China PR must be asked to demonstrate that market economy conditions prevail in 
their industry producing the like product with regard to production and sale of the product under consideration. 
It has been stated by the applicants that in case the responding Chinese producers are not able to demonstrate 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 6l 


38. 


39. 


G.3.2. 
40. 


that their cost and price information are market-driven, the normal value should be calculated in terms of 
provisions of Para 7 and 8 of Annexure-I to the Rules. 


In the present case, the only cooperating producer/exporter from China has not claimed market economy 
treatment. Accordingly, the normal value has been determined in accordance with paragraph 7 of Annexure I to 
the Rules, which states as follows: 


“In case of imports from non-market economy countries, normal value shall be determined on the basis 
of the price or constructed value in the market economy third country, or the price from such a third 
country to other countries, including India or where it is not possible, on any other reasonable basis, 
including the price actually paid or payable in India for the like product, duly adjusted if necessary, to 
include a reasonable profit margin. An appropriate market economy third country shall be selected by 
the designated authority in a reasonable manner, keeping in view the level of development of the 
country concerned and the product in question, and due account shall be taken of any reliable 
information made available at the time of selection. Account shall be taken within time limits, where 
appropriate, of the investigation if any made in similar matter in respect of any other market economy 
third country. The parties to the investigation shall be informed without any unreasonable delay the 
aforesaid selection of the market economy third country and shall be given a reasonable period of time 
to offer their comments.” 


In the present case, there is no evidence of price or constructed value prevailing in a market economy third 
country brought forward by any interested party. Further, the product does not have a dedicated tariff code, to 
permit consideration of price of exports from a market economy third country to other countries, including 
India. The Authority has therefore, determined normal value as per the price payable in India, based on cost of 
production of the applicants, duly adjusted for selling, general and administrative expenses and reasonable 
profits.

previous investigations have continued to export the goods to India, but have chosen not to participate in the 
present review. The Authority accordingly notes that the export price of the cooperating producer is 
representative only of a very small volume exported by them, and cannot be regarded as representative of 
imports of the subject goods from the subject country as a whole.

H. DETERMINATION OF INJURY AND CAUSAL LINK 
Submissions by other interested parties 


The following submissions have been made by the other interested parties with regard to injury, likelihood of 
recurrence or continuation of injury and causal link. 


i. The volume parameters of the domestic industry have improved: production of the PUC increased from 
5,29,77 MT to 6,9,096 MT (7%), domestic sales from 4,37,475 MT to 5,5,983 MT, capacity 
utilisation from 46% to 49%, productivity per day from ,472 MT to ,720 MT and per employee from 
8l MT to 205 MT, while inventories declined from 33 to 28 days of production. The market share of 
the domestic industry remained stable. 


il. The low capacity utilisation is attributable to the domestic industry’s own capacity expansion decisions 
and not to imports. 


ili. Imports from other countries are 9-24% cheaper than imports from China PR and yet have not displaced 
Chinese imports, showing that demand for Chinese goods is driven by supply reliability and product 
range, and not by price. 


iv. The export price of the domestic industry has declined by 33 index points, nearly twice the decline in its 
domestic price (8 index points), which shows that the price decline is due to global market conditions 
and not due to subject imports. 


Submissions by the domestic industry 


The following submissions have been made by the domestic industry with regard to the injury, likelihood of 
continuation of injury and causal link. 


i; The volume of the subject imports has increased over the injury period and have almost doubled when 
compared to the previous sunset review, despite duties. 


il. The volume of subject imports has increased despite duties, while the volume of imports from other 
countries has declined. 


ill. | The domestic industry has been forced to reduce its price due to decline in landed price despite the 
presence of anti-dumping duty, resulting in decline in its profitability. 


iv. Although cost of sales declined over the injury period, rate of decline in selling price of the domestic 
industry was higher than the decline in its costs. 


Vv. Despite increase in demand for the subject goods over the injury period, the domestic industry was able 
to utilize only less than half of its capacity, despite having sufficient capacity, resulting in high fixed 
costs. 

vi. | The domestic industry suffered loss of market share despite large unutilised capacities and no demand 
supply-gap. 


Vii. Due to continued dumping, the domestic industry has suffered a sharp decline in its profitability.

H.3. Examination by the Authority 
48. The Authority has taken note of the submissions of the interested parties. The injury analysis has been made in 
accordance with Rule |] and Annexure-II to the Rules, having regard to the volume of dumped imports, their 
effect on prices in the domestic market for like articles, and the consequent impact on the domestic producers 
of such articles. In a sunset review, where the anti-dumping duty is in force, the Authority is required to 
examine whether injury has continued despite the duty, and whether there is likelihood of continuation or 
recurrence of injury if the duty were allowed to cease. 
H.3.. Assessment of demand/apparent consumption

as the sum of the domestic sales of the domestic industry and other Indian producers and imports from all 
sources. The domestic industry has not captively consumed the subject goods.

The Authority notes that the volume of subject imports has increased in absolute terms by 53% over the injury 
period, in spite of the anti-dumping duty in force, and at a rate higher than the growth in demand (23%). The 
subject imports have also increased in relation to Indian production and consumption, and their share in total 
imports increased to 74% over the injury period. Despite no demand-supply gap in the country, production 
capacities by the domestic industry are significantly under-utilised, and domestic industry is selling the product 
materially below a price that would allow reasonable return on investment to the domestic industry (the returns 
earned by the domestic industry in the POI was quite low). While the sales of the domestic industry increased 
only by 7%, dumped imports increased by 53%. The domestic industry contended that the import prices are 
being aligned to the benchmark and imports are being made practically without payment of ADD, resulting in 
increase in imports. 


Price effect of dumped imports 


The price effect of the dumped imports has been examined in terms of Para (ii) of Annexure-II to the Customs 
Tariff (Identification, Assessment and Collection of Anti-Dumping Duty on Dumped Articles and for 
Determination of Injury) Rules, 995, which provides as follows: 


“While examining the volume of dumped imports, the said authority shall consider whether there has 
been a significant increase in the dumped imports either in absolute terms or relative to production or 
consumption in India, and with regard to the effect of the dumped imports on prices as referred to in 
sub-rule (2) of rule 6 the designated authority shall consider whether there has been a significant 
price undercutting by the dumped imports as compared with the price of like product in India, or 
whether the effect of such imports is otherwise to depress prices to a significant degree or prevent price 
increase which otherwise would have occurred to a significant degree.” 


A reading of the above provision shows that the price effect of the dumped imports is to be examined with 
reference to three independent and disjunctive parameters, namely (a) significant price undercutting, (b) 
significant price depression, and (c) significant price suppression, i.e. prevention of price increases which 
would otherwise have occurred. The said parameters are connected by the disjunctive expression “or”, and the 
existence of any one of them is sufficient to establish that the dumped imports have had an adverse effect on 
the prices of the domestic industry. The absence of price undercutting in a given period does not, therefore, 
preclude a finding of price depression or price suppression. 


Price undercutting 


The price undercutting has been determined by comparing the landed price of the subject imports with the net 
sales realisation of the domestic industry, PCN-wise, for the POI. The landed price has been determined on the 
basis of the CIF value as per DG Systems data, with the addition of the applicable basic customs duty and 
social welfare surcharge.

payment of ADD and the imported product has been resold in the market at a price lower than the import 
prices. The subject imports have thus depressed the prices of the domestic industry, as a result of which the 
margin between the selling price and cost of sales of the domestic industry fell sharply.

capacity; factors affecting domestic prices; the magnitude of the margin of dumping; actual and potential 
negative effects on cash flow, inventories, employment, wages, growth, and ability to raise capital investments.

Magnitude of dumping 


The dumping margins determined for the subject country are positive and significant, as set out in Section G 
above. 


Magnitude of injury margin 


The Authority has determined the non-injurious price for the domestic industry on the basis of the principles 
laid down in Annexure-III to the Rules, as amended. The NIP of the product under consideration has been 
determined by adopting the verified information/data relating to the cost of production for the POI. The NIP 
has been considered for comparison with the landed price of the subject imports for calculating the injury 
margin. For determination of the NIP, the best utilisation of raw materials and utilities has been considered 
over the injury period; the best utilisation of production capacity over the injury period has been considered; 
extraordinary or non-recurring expenses have been excluded; and a reasonable return (pre-tax @ 22%) on the 
average capital employed for the product under consideration has been allowed, as prescribed in Annexure-III. 


The landed price for JSC has been determined on the basis of its verified CIF export price, with the addition of 
basic customs duty and social welfare surcharge at the effective rates applicable to its transactions as recorded 
in DG Systems data. The landed price for all other producers/exporters has been determined on the basis of DG 
Systems data. 


Based on the landed price and non-injurious price determined as above, the injury margin has been determined 
by the Authority and the same is provided in the table below:

Analysis of injury 


The Authority notes that the volume of subject imports has increased in absolute terms and in relation to 
production and consumption in India despite the anti-dumping duty in force. The imports are largely of hot 
finished line pipe, which is the majority product for the domestic industry. The price undercutting on the basis 
of weighted average landed price of imports is negative. The domestic industry has however contended that the 
imports have been made primarily by the traders, import prices have been aligned to the benchmark notified by 


[भाग [खण्ड ] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 69 


I.. 
79. 


2. 
80. 


the Authority and the imported product has been resold at a price lower than the import price. Resultantly, the 
domestic industry has been forced to sell the product at lower prices. The subject imports have depressed the 
prices of the domestic industry, with the domestic industry forced to reduce its prices at a rate much higher than 
the decline in its costs. Although the domestic industry has registered growth in production and sales, it has lost 
market share and has operated at less than 50% capacity utilisation throughout the injury period even when 
domestic industry could have catered to this demand in the Country. Performance of the domestic industry has 
very sharply deteriorated in respect of profits, cash profits and return on capital. The return on capital 
employed is at unsustainable levels for long term operations of the domestic industry. Thus, the Authority 
concludes that the domestic industry has continued to suffer injury during the POI despite the anti-dumping 
duty in force and its performance has deteriorated in the current injury period. 


I. LIKELIHOOD OF CONTINUATION OR RECURRENCE OF DUMPING AND INJURY 
Submissions by other interested parties 


The submissions made by the other interested parties are as below. 
i. JSC’s exports to India have not increased over the injury period. 


il. JSC’s installed capacity and utilization has remained stable, leaving no meaningful surplus capacity for 
diversion to India. 


ili. JSC’s export price to India is about 30% higher than its domestic price in China PR, and India is a 
premium market for JSC. 


iv. The data relied upon by the domestic industry is aggregate, macro-level data which does not address the 
conduct of JSC. The projected loss scenario is speculative and premised on unsubstantiated 
assumptions. 


Submissions by the domestic industry 


The submissions made by the domestic industry are as below. 


if The exporters in China have a history of dumping, evident from the significant dumping margins 
calculated in the original investigation and the first sunset review. 


il. The rate of increase in volume of imports has outpaced the rate of increase in the demand in the country, 
which indicates that in case of expiry of duties, the subject imports would flood the Indian market. 


ili. Cessation of duty will lead to significant increase in dumping of subject goods in the Indian market 
since the producers in China have large production capacities. 


iv. The producers in China have significant underutilised capacities. Even a small increase in the capacity 
utilization of idle capacities can capture the entire demand in India and thus, cessation of duty will 
likely lead to diversion of subject goods into India. 


Vv. The producers in China are largely export oriented with half of the total production capacity intended 
for exports due to limited demand in China. 


vi. Various countries such as USA, European Union, Canada, Brazil, Mexico and Ukraine have imposed 
trade remedial measures against imports from China, which has restricted market access for Chinese 
goods. 


vii. Countries including USA, European Union and Canada have also imposed other tariff and non-tariff 
measures leading to further restriction of available market for the Chinese producers. 


Vill. Despite anti-dumping duties, the domestic industry is facing intense price pressure and declining 
profitability and returns, which is likely to aggravate if the duty is allowed to expire. 


ix. Given the landed price of imports, in the event of cessation of duty, the domestic industry would be 
forced to reduce its price and would incur losses, with a negative return on investment. 


X. Expiry of duties is likely to intensify dumping and cause severe injury to the already fragile domestic 
industry.

Examination by the Authority 


The present investigation is a sunset review, wherein the Authority is required to examine whether the expiry 
of the anti-dumping duty is likely to lead to continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. The Rules do 
not prescribe a specific methodology for the likelihood analysis. The Authority has examined the likelihood 
having regard to the factors in Article |l.3 of the Anti-Dumping Agreement and Section 9A(5) of the Act, and 
the factors referred to in Article 3.7 of the Agreement, namely the rate of increase of dumped imports, freely 
disposable capacity of the exporters, prices of imports and inventories, to the extent relevant. The Authority has 
examined all the relevant information brought on record by the domestic industry and the other interested 
parties and has noted as below. 


With regards to the arguments that JSC has not witnessed any large capacity expansions, does not have any idle 
capacities and its exports realization is higher than its domestic prices, the Authority notes that the likelihood 
determination is made with respect to the subject country as a whole. The data of a single exporter accounting 
for about 5% of the subject imports cannot by itself negate the likelihood established on the basis of the 
country-level evidence. 


Continued dumping despite duties 


The dumping margins determined for the POI are positive and significant, both for the responding exporter and 
for the other producers/exporters. The dumping margins in the original investigation and first sunset review 
were also significant. The exporters have thus continued to export at dumped prices over a prolonged period, 
and it is likely that dumping would continue, or intensify, in the absence of duty. 


Investigation Dumping margin (Range) 


Original investigation 20 - 30% 


First sunset review 25 - 35% 


Increase in volume of subject imports 


The volume of subject imports increased by 53% over the injury period, at a rate higher than the growth in 
demand. Further, the volume of imports in the POI exceeded the entire volume of subject imports in the POI of 
the first sunset review, which covered a wider product scope. The subject imports have thus continued to enter 
in increasing volumes, indicating a likelihood of further increase upon cessation of ADD. 


Investigation Period Volume of imports (MT) 


First sunset review with wider 


product scope Apr 209 - Sept 2020 73,628 


Second sunset review Oct 2024 — Sept 2025 ,53,069 


It is also seen that 88% of total imports are of hot finished pipes. These are the majority products for the 
domestic industry. Thus, there is increased likelihood of increase in imports in a short duration in the event of 
cessation of ADD. 


Rate of increase in imports 


It is seen that the volume of imports has continued to increase over the period. Further, such increase in the 
volume of imports is higher than rate of increase in demand. Whereas demand for the product increased by 
23%, imports increased by 53%. In case duties are allowed to expire, the volume of imports would not only 
continue to increase, but also would intensify significantly and would further erode the market share of the 
domestic industry. It is noted that the exporters from the subject country have continued to dump the subject 
goods in India despite anti-dumping duties. Further, as can be seen from above, the dumping margin for the 
exporters was significant in the original investigation and the first sunset review, indicating a tendency to 
dump. Thus, in the event of cessation of duties, the dumping is likely to continue and / or intensify.

I.3.4. Surplus capacities available with Chinese producers 
87. The applicants have reported that producers in the subject country have large production capacities, to the tune 
of 25.65 million MT as per market research agency report. Further, the applicants have submitted that certain 
producers have individual capacities higher than the demand in India. It is noted that the production capacities 
in the subject country are much higher than the demand in India. Such capacities can be used to dump the 
subject goods in case of expiry of duties. The Authority notes the argument of JSC that its export realisations 
are better than domestic market realisation, showing that Indian market is a lucrative market for the Chinese 
producers. Thus, cessation of ADD would not only allow the Chinese producers to utilise their production 
capacities, but also divert their current sales to the Indian market. 
I.3.5. Significantly underutilized capacities 
88. The applicants have further submitted that the production volume of the Chinese producers is much lower than 
the capacities available with them. It is seen that the producers in the subject country have large idle capacities 
which can be utilized for increasing exports to India.

I.3.7. Trade remedial measures in third countries 
90. The Authority notes that exports of the subject goods from China PR face anti-dumping and/or countervailing 


measures in the EU, Canada, Brazil, Mexico, Ukraine and the USA. As a result of such duties, the third country 
markets are limited for the producers, making India a lucrative market.

I.3.8. Other measures imposed by third countries 
9. In addition to the above, it is noted that the USA has imposed tariffs of 50% on steel and steel products under 
Section 232, the EU has introduced the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism, and Canada has introduced 
additional steel tariffs. With the major import markets restricted, India, with a growing demand, is an attractive 
market for diversion of Chinese exports in the event of cessation of duties. 
I.3.9. The situation of the domestic industry continues to remain fragile 
92. It is seen that the subject goods have continued to be dumped in the Indian market despite duties. The dumped 
goods account for significant share in the market. As a result, the profitability parameters of the domestic 
industry have very significantly declined over the injury period. Further, the domestic industry is faced with 
significant under utilisation of its production capacities. Thus, if the duties are allowed to expire, the subject 
imports will have a further adverse impact on the performance of the domestic industry. 
I.3.0. Likely impact on the domestic industry 
93. The applicants have claimed that despite anti-dumping duties, the subject imports have created a strain on the 
prices of the domestic industry, forcing it to sell its product at the cost of its profitability. However, domestic 
industry contended, if the duties expire, the domestic industry would be forced to further reduce prices, given 
that imported product is being sold significantly at lower prices than the imported price. This would further 
deteriorate the profitability of the industry. It is seen that considering the current prices of the domestic 
industry, reduction of even !% in prices shall lead to financial losses to the domestic industry. 
I.3.ll. Analysis of likelihood of continuation / recurrence of dumping, and injury in the event of cessation of 
ADD 
94. On the basis of the above, the Authority concludes that there is a likelihood of continuation of dumping and 
injury to the domestic industry in the event of cessation of the anti-dumping duty on imports of the subject 
goods from China PR. The domestic industry is already suffering deterioration in its performance despite 
existing ADD. The performance of the domestic industry is likely to deteriorate further. 
J. CAUSAL LINK AND NON-ATTRIBUTION ANALYSIS 
95. As per the Rules, the Authority is required to, inter alia, examine any known factors other than dumped imports 


which are injuring or are likely to cause injury to the domestic industry, so that the injury caused by these other 
factors may not be attributed to the dumped imports. While the present investigation is a sunset review 
investigation and causal link has already been examined in original investigation, the Authority has examined 
whether other known factors listed under the Rules could have contributed to the injury to the domestic 
industry, so that injury caused by such factors is not attributed to the dumped imports.

Non-attribution analysis 


It was examined whether other factors listed under the Rules could have contributed or are likely to contribute 
to the injury suffered by the domestic industry. The following is noted in this regard. 


Volume and price of imports from third countries 


Other than imports from China PR, there are imports from UAE, Saudi Arabia, Singapore and USA are above 
de-minimis levels. Imports from rest of the other countries are below de-minimis levels. 


The Authority notes that the present investigation is a sunset review under Section 9A(5) of the Customs Tariff 
Act, 975, read with Rule 23 of the Anti-Dumping Rules and Article |.3 of the Anti-Dumping Agreement. Its 
scope is set by the measure under review: the anti-dumping duty in force on imports of the subject goods from 
the subject country, China PR. The Authority is required to determine whether expiry of that duty would be 
likely to lead to continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. 


The Authority notes that during the POI, the subject goods were also imported in significant volumes from 
USA, Saudi Arabia, Singapore and UAE at prices below those of the subject imports. These countries are not 
covered by the measure under review or by the present investigation. No application has been filed before the 
Authority on imports from them. The Authority therefore has not examined, and makes no determination on, 
whether imports from these countries are dumped or injurious. If the domestic industry considers that imports 
from any of these countries are dumped and are causing injury, it may file a duly substantiated application 
under Rule 5 of the Rules. Any such application will be considered separately on its merits. 


The domestic industry has submitted that neither Singapore nor the UAE has facilities to produce the subject 
goods. It contends that imports declared as originating from these countries are in fact Chinese goods 
transshipped through them to avoid the duty in force. The Authority notes that whether imports from these 
countries circumvent the duty in force is a separate matter, governed by Rule 25 of the Rules. 


Contraction in demand 


. The demand for the subject goods has increased by 23% over the injury period and is not expected to decline. 


Injury to the domestic industry is, therefore, not likely to be caused due to any contraction in demand. 


Changes in the pattern of consumption 


. There is no evidence on record of any change in the pattern of consumption of the subject goods, which has 


caused or is likely to cause injury to the domestic industry. 


Trade restrictive practices and competition between domestic and foreign producers 


. There is no trade restrictive practice which could have contributed, or is likely to contribute, to the injury. The 


import of the subject goods is freely permissible. 


Developments in technology 


. No interested party has submitted evidence of any significant change in technology that could have caused 


injury to the domestic industry. 
Export performance of the domestic industry 


The injury analysis has been carried out with reference to the domestic operations of the domestic industry 
only. JSC has contended that the export price of the domestic industry declined faster than its domestic price, 
indicating global price trends. The Authority notes that the information on injury relates to domestic 
operations, and that even with the decline in global prices, the landed price of the subject imports was below 
the cost of sales of the domestic industry in India during the POI. The fall in export prices cannot explain the 
price depression suffered in the domestic market. 


Performance of other products 
The data considered by the Authority relates only to the product under consideration. 
Productivity 


The productivity of the domestic industry has improved over the injury period and cannot be a cause of injury.

Causal link 


The following parameters establish causal link between the dumped imports and injury to the domestic 
industry. 


a. Despite anti-dumping duties in force, the subject imports have continued to enter the domestic market at 
dumped prices. 


b. The subject imports have increased in absolute terms and in relation to domestic production and 
consumption over the injury period. 


c. Asaresult, the market share of the domestic industry has declined over the injury period, while that of the 
subject imports has increased over the period. 


d. The domestic industry was forced to reduce its selling price at a rate higher than decline in costs, in order 
to compete with the dumped imports. 


e. Asaresult, the domestic industry faced significant decline in its profits and its cash profits declined. 
f. This resulted in the domestic industry earning very low returns on its investment. 


g. While the capacities have remained stable and production has increased, the domestic industry operated at 
very low capacity utilisation. 


In view of the above, the Authority concludes that the injury suffered by the domestic industry is on account of 
the dumped imports from the subject country, and that the other known factors have not caused injury to the 
domestic industry. 


K. INDIAN INDUSTRY’S INTEREST AND OTHER ISSUES 
Submissions of other interested parties 
No submissions have been made by any importer / user / consumer with regard to the Indian industry’s interest. 
Submissions of domestic industry 
The following submissions have been made in this regard. 


i. The continuation of anti-dumping duty is in public interest and will have no adverse impact on 
downstream users or end-consumers. 


il. The absence of participation by users or consumers indicates that the anti-dumping duties have not had 
any significant adverse impact on them and that continuation of duties remains in the public interest. 


ili. The subject goods are majorly used in large scale refineries or boilers which are greenfield or 
brownfield capacity establishments, wherein they constitute only a negligible share in the total cost of 
such large-scale projects. 


iv. Continuation of duties would have a minuscule impact of about 0.0% on the total project cost. 


Vv. The subject goods have a long lifespan of 25-30 years, and the impact of continued duties would be 
negligible when spread over the life of the project. 


vi. The domestic industry holds a total capacity of 7,65,000 MT as against a demand of 7,95,620 MT. 
Hence, there is no demand-supply gap in the country. 


Vii. Rashmi Seamless Limited has announced capacity additions of ,50,000 MT in their second phase and a 
further addition of 2,50,000 MT in their third phase. Expiry of anti-dumping duties would discourage 
investment and jeopardize planned capacity expansions by the Indian industry. 


Vili. A strong and financially healthy domestic seamless pipe industry is critical to support India's energy 
security, infrastructure development, and strategic national priorities. 


ix. | The domestic industry has made substantial investments in capacity expansion and import substitution 
in line with Atmanirbhar Bharat and Make in India initiatives. The domestic industry has developed and 
indigenized several critical high-value products. Hence, it requires continued support. 


X. Expiry of duties would undermine the substantial investments made by the domestic industry in 
capacity expansion, product development, and obtaining critical technical approvals.

Xi. Preservation of a healthy domestic industry will support substantial direct and indirect employment

Examination by the Authority 


The Authority notes that the primary objective of anti-dumping duties is to rectify the injury inflicted upon the 
domestic industry by the unjust trade practices of dumping, thereby fostering an environment of open and 
equitable competition in the Indian market. This is not merely a regulatory measure, but a matter of national 
interest. The imposition of anti-dumping measures is not designed to curtail imports. Rather, it is a mechanism 
to ensure a level playing field. The Authority acknowledges that the continuation of anti-dumping duties may 
influence the price levels of the product in India. However, it is crucial to note that the essence of fair 
competition in the Indian market will remain unscathed by the continuation of these measures. Far from 
diminishing competition, anti-dumping measures serve to prevent the accrual of unfair advantages through 
dumping practices. They safeguard the consumers' access to a broad selection of the subject goods. Thus, anti- 
dumping duties are not a hindrance but a facilitator of fair-trade practices. In this regard, the Hon'ble Supreme 
Court in Reliance Industries Ltd. v. Designated Authority & Ors. observed that dumping may provide 
transitory benefits to consumers in the short term on account of lower prices, but in the long run may adversely 
affect domestic industry and competition in the market. 


The Authority issued the initiation notification, inviting views from all interested parties including importers, 
users and consumers. An Economic Interest Questionnaire was also prescribed to allow various stakeholders, 
including the domestic industry, producers/exporters and importers/users/consumers to provide relevant 
information concerning the present investigation, including the possible effect of anti-dumping duty on their 
operations. 


The Authority notes that no importer, user or consumer of the subject goods has participated in the present 
investigation or filed an economic interest questionnaire response. 


The domestic industry has submitted that the subject goods are capital goods with a life of 25-30 years used 
predominantly by large corporations in hydrocarbon, refinery, power and boiler sectors. Further, the domestic 
industry has claimed that users are not part of the MSME sector, and are majorly large-scale corporations. It is 
noted that the product is purchased and used at the time of setting up large-scale refinery or boiler projects. The 
product under consideration accounts for a very negligible share in the total cost of such large-scale projects. 
Considering the same, continuation of duties would have negligible impact of only 0.09% on the costs of the 
downstream users, as can be seen from the table below. Further, since the goods have a life span of up to 30 
years, the annual impact spread over 3 decades would be even less.

6. It is noted that there is no demand-supply gap in the country necessitating dumped imports into the country. 
The Authority notes that the domestic industry has significant capacities which are sufficient to meet the entire 
present and foreseeable demand in the country. There are a number of producers of the subject goods in the

The Authority further notes that none of the users have participated in the present review to claim / demonstrate 
that imposition of duties in the past has negatively impacted their operations. It is noted that present duties have 
not adversely impacted the user industry, which is evident from the absence of any user participation. Thus, 
continuation of duties is also not likely to have any adverse impact on the downstream users. In contrast, the 
anti-dumping duties have created a fair market condition in the country, which has enabled the Indian industry 
to sustain its production and sales volumes. Thus, continuation of duties would allow the Indian industry to 
remain healthy and further improve its economic parameters, in absence of any unfairly priced imports in the 
market creating pressure on the domestic prices. 


POST-DISCLOSURE COMMENTS 


The Authority circulated the disclosure statement containing all essential facts under consideration for making 
the final recommendations to the Central Government, to all interested parties on 4" October 2026. The 
Authority has examined all the post-disclosure comments made by the interested parties in these final findings 
to the extent deemed relevant. Any submission which was merely a reproduction of the previous submissions, 
and which had been adequately examined by the Authority has not been repeated for the sake of brevity. 


Submissions by the other interested parties 
The other interested parties have made the following submissions post issuance of the disclosure statement: 


a. The Authority should confirm the dumping margin in range of 5-l5% and negative injury margin 
determined for Jiangsu Chengde Steel Tube Share Co. Limited in the final findings. 


b. Section 9A(5) of the Customs Tariff Act, Rule 23 and 47 of the Anti-Dumping Rules and Paragraph 
7.29 of the Manual of Operating Practices clearly provided that duties can be modified or revised in a 
review. 


९. Simply extending the original duty without giving effect to the margins found in this review would 
make the review pointless. 


d. The CESTAT has, in various cases, held that the Authority may recommend a duty higher or lower than 
the original duty in a sunset review to remedy the injury, or else, the purpose of a review would be 
defeated, which includes (a) Robin Resources vs. Designated Authority (20l6 (342) E.L.T. 8); (७) 
Shanghai Fortune Chemical Company Ltd. vs. Designated Authority (20l6 (342) E.L.T. 578); and (०) 
Indian Graphite Mfrs. Asscn. vs. Designated Authority (2006 (99) E.L.T. 722). This has also been the 
practice of the Authority in many cases. 


e. Continuing the original rate and form of duty for Jiangsu Chengde, despite the fresh margins found in 
this review, would over-protect the domestic industry, and would go beyond what is “necessary’’ to 
remove injury under Rule 23(.) read with Rule 7. 


Submissions by the domestic industry 
The domestic industry has made the following submissions post issuance of the disclosure statement: 


a. There is a need to modify the quantum and form of current duties in place since the reference price duty 
is based on the cost and price data for the period of investigation in the original investigation, which is 
more than 0 years old. The duties have been ineffective in providing relief to the industry. 


b. The volume of imports has increased in the current period, despite anti-dumping duties in place, 
particularly of hot-rolled line pipes which is the most basic product. 


९. Chinese exporters have aligned their prices to the reference price. The exporters have quoted a price in 
the range of USD 650-850/MT, which is well below the current NIP, as can be seen from the price 
quotations of Chinese producers as well as the invoices of re-sale of products by traders in the market. 
However, the import prices declared to Indian customs are at or just above the reference price. 


d. The domestic industry’s current NIP is higher than the reference price as well as landed price, and thus, 
as long as the landed price stays just above the reference price, imports will keep causing injury. 


e. The prices of steel billets declined over the injury period and were lowest during the period of 
investigation but increased thereafter, as can be seen from the break-up of costs submitted. A 
benchmark based on such prices would not reflect the domestic industry’s current costs or fair price, and 
would again become ineffective.

f. Despite anti-dumping duties, the domestic industry has continued to suffer injury and its profitability

g. The domestic industry earlier filed an application for mid-term review for a change in the form and

h. Section 9A(5) read with Rules 23(/B), 23(3) and 7()(b) of the Anti-Dumping Rules, allow the 
Authority to modify duties in a sunset review and recommend a duty that would remove the injury to 
the domestic industry. The CESTAT has upheld this position in various cases, such as (a) Association of 
Synthetics Fibre Industry vs. Union of India (20l0 (256) E.L.T. 745); (b) Thai Acrylic Fibre Co. Ltd. 
vs. Designated Authority (20l0 (253) E.L.T. 564); (c) Robin Resources vs. Designated Authority (206

reviews based on the facts and circumstances of each case. 


j. The subject goods are mainly used in refinery boiler systems, heat exchangers, mechanical, structural 
and general engineering applications. Even if duties upto 0% are imposed, the impact of such duties in

duties are allowed to expire. 


Examination by the Authority 


The Authority has examined the post-disclosure submissions made by the domestic industry and the other 
interested parties and notes that some of the comments have already been examined suitably and addressed 
adequately in the relevant paras of the final findings. The issues raised for the first time in the post-disclosure 
comments/submissions by the interested parties and the domestic industry, which are considered relevant by 
the Authority, and which have not been already examined above, are examined below. 


The domestic industry has reiterated its request for change in the form of duties in order to remedy the 
continued injury being caused by the dumped imports. The domestic industry has submitted evidence in the 
form of price quotations, invoices issued by traders, consumption prices of the raw material, prescribed legal 
position and past practice of the Authority. Jiangsu Chengde Steel Tube Share Co. Limited has also sought 
modification of the form of duty and fixation of ADD based on dumping and injury margin found by the 
authority. 


As noted above, the current duty in the form of reference price was determined on the basis of the cost and 
price conditions prevailing in the POI of the original investigation. (Jan-Dec 20!4) Despite duties, the volume 
of imports has been increasing in the current injury period, as the subject imports have continued to enter at 
prices just above the reference price for the PCNs commanding predominant share in imports. Further, the 
performance of the domestic industry has deteriorated in respect of profits, cash profits and return on capital 
employed. The domestic industry is suffering from gross under-utilisation of production capacities, while 
imports have been increasing. The increase in import volumes is in respect of the product type which is not 
only being produced by all the three domestic producers, but also which constitutes majority share in the 
product profile of these domestic producers. Thus, considering the factual matrix of the case wherein the 
domestic industry is suffering deterioration in its price parameters and under-utilisation of production 
capacities, while import volumes are rising, the Authority deems it appropriate to modify the form of duty from 
reference price to a fixed duty expressed in US$ per MT, being the lesser of the dumping margin and injury 
margin determined in the present review, so as to remove the continued injury suffered by the domestic 
industry. 


The domestic industry has submitted that continuation of duties would not adversely impact any downstream 
user. The domestic industry has submitted that even if duties upto |0% are imposed, the impact of such duties 
would be negligible on various downstream application. Further, since the goods have a life span of up to 30 
years, the annual impact spread over 3 decades would be even less.
